                - link "37570818" [ref=e102] [cursor=pointer]:

          - region "Article content" [ref=e103]:
            - generic [ref=e104]:
              - generic [ref=e105]:
                - heading "Abstract" [level=2] [ref=e106]: Abstract
                - paragraph [ref=e107]: Enzymes are essential catalysts for various chemical reactions in biological systems and often rely on metal ions or cofactors to stabilize their structure or perform functions. Improving enzyme performance has always been an important direction of protein engineering. In recent years, various artificial small molecules have been successfully used in enzyme engineering. The types of enzymatic reactions and metabolic pathways in cells can be expanded by the incorporation of these artificial small molecules either as cofactors or as building blocks of proteins and nucleic acids, which greatly promotes the development and application of biotechnology. In this review, we summarized research on artificial small molecules including biological metal cluster mimics, coenzyme analogs (mNADs), designer cofactors, non-natural nucleotides (XNAs), and non-natural amino acids (nnAAs), focusing on their design, synthesis, and applications as well as the current challenges in synthetic biology.
                - paragraph [ref=e109]:
                  - strong [ref=e110]: "Keywords:"
                  - text: artificial small molecules, biological metal cluster mimics, coenzyme analogs, designer cofactors, non-natural nucleotides, non-natural amino acids, synthetic biology
              - generic [ref=e111]:
                - heading "1. Introduction" [level=2] [ref=e112]: 1. Introduction
                - paragraph [ref=e113]:
                  - text: Enzymes are extremely important catalysts and play a wide range of functions in the biological system. However, natural enzymes frequently cannot meet the catalytic requirements compatible with the metabolism of a chassis host in vivo, so engineering of the enzyme needs to be carried out on the cofactor specificity, substrate scope, and robustness [
                  - link "1" [ref=e114] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B1-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. With the development of synthetic biology, the engineering of enzymes is no longer limited to mutations of natural amino acids at different sites but increasingly involves the development of the low-cost, stable, and better-performing artificial small molecules. These artificial small molecules can serve as cofactors or building blocks of biomacromolecules, performing important biological functions, enhancing the performance of the enzymes, and expanding the types of biological metabolic pathways and biocatalytic reactions. A series of enzymes containing artificial small molecules have been obtained with improved activity, stability, stereoselectivity, and cofactor specificity. Metalloenzymes are a class of widely distributed enzymes that are frequently utilized for various challenging catalytic reactions. Important hydrogenases for biological hydrogen production, including [Ni-Fe] hydrogenase, [Fe-Fe] hydrogenase, and [Fe] hydrogenase ["
                  - link "2" [ref=e115] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B2-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], contain complex metal clusters and various cofactors. Researchers have made many efforts in metal center substitution, design, and incorporating artificial metal clusters as cofactors such as [Ni-Ru], [Ni-Mn], and NiRd ["
                  - link "3" [ref=e116] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B3-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "4" [ref=e117] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B4-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. The most widely used metalloprotein, hemoprotein, has successfully undergone the engineering of its cofactor heme, and designed artificial heme cofactors have been successfully used for cytochrome P450 enzymes ["
                  - link "5" [ref=e118] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B5-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. Many redox enzymes require cofactors (coenzymes) to complete catalytic reactions with different cofactor preferences. To address problems such as an imbalance between supply and demand, a high production cost, and the low stability of cofactors in metabolic processes, non-natural coenzymes (mNADs) such as nicotinamide cytosine dinucleotide (NCD) and nicotinamide mononucleotide (NMN) have been biosynthesized ["
                  - link "6" [ref=e119] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B6-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "7" [ref=e120] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B7-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "8" [ref=e121] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B8-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. Combined with random mutagenesis, semi-rational design, and rational design, the mutant enzymes specifically using mNADs as coenzymes have been realized ["
                  - link "9" [ref=e122] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B9-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "10" [ref=e123] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B10-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "11" [ref=e124] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B11-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. Using designer cofactors, P450 enzymes have been engineered to catalyze novel reactions for non-natural substrates ["
                  - link "12" [ref=e125] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B12-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. In addition, new components such as non-natural nucleotides (XNAs) and non-natural amino acids (nnAAs) are increasingly being used in semi-synthetic organism creation ["
                  - link "13" [ref=e126] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B13-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], improving enzyme properties and functions, and solving challenging biological problems ["
                  - link "14" [ref=e127] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B14-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]."
                - paragraph [ref=e128]: Research on biological metal cluster mimics, coenzyme analogs, designer cofactors, and non-natural nucleotides and amino acids has made a lot of progress in recent years. Currently, the synthesis and engineering of artificial small molecules are becoming increasingly sophisticated, which not only promote the exploration and development of cellular metabolic pathways but also play an important role in synthetic biology and metabolism engineering. This review aims to summarize the design, synthesis, and application of artificial small molecules in synthetic biology and discuss current challenges and future directions in research.
              - generic [ref=e129]:
                - heading "2. Biological Metal Cluster Mimics" [level=2] [ref=e130]: 2. Biological Metal Cluster Mimics
                - paragraph [ref=e131]:
                  - text: Some of the most challenging chemical reactions in nature, such as water splitting in photosynthesis and the reduction of N
                  - subscript [ref=e132]: "2"
                  - text: to NH
                  - subscript [ref=e133]: "3"
                  - text: in biological nitrogen fixation, are catalyzed by enzymes with metal cofactors under mild conditions without a high temperature or high pressure. The engineering of these enzymes is of great interest in both biofuel and biochemical production. Many researchers have produced artificial metalloenzymes with new functions by reusing the natural protein [
                  - link "15" [ref=e134] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B15-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "] or building entirely new artificial metalloproteins from scratch ["
                  - link "16" [ref=e135] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B16-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]."
                - generic [ref=e136]:
                  - heading "2.1. Metal Clusters of Artificial Hydrogenases" [level=3] [ref=e137]
                  - paragraph [ref=e138]:
                    - text: Due to the negative impact of non-renewable resources such as fossil fuels on the environment, the development of sustainable energy sources is particularly important. H
                    - subscript [ref=e139]: "2"
                    - text: ", as a clean alternative fuel, can greatly reduce atmospheric pollution and is expected to become a sustainable green energy carrier and storage method ["
                    - link "17" [ref=e140] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B17-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. As important enzymes in biological hydrogen production, hydrogenases can reversibly convert H"
                    - subscript [ref=e141]: "2"
                    - text: into protons and electrons [
                    - link "18" [ref=e142] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B18-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. According to their different metal ions in the active centers, hydrogenases are classified into [Ni-Fe] hydrogenases, [Fe-Fe] hydrogenases, and [Fe] hydrogenases ["
                    - link "2" [ref=e143] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B2-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. The first two types of hydrogenases have different sensitivities to O"
                    - subscript [ref=e144]: "2"
                    - text: . [Ni-Fe] hydrogenase is reversibly inactivated by O
                    - subscript [ref=e145]: "2"
                    - text: ", while [Fe-Fe] hydrogenase is highly sensitive to O"
                    - subscript [ref=e146]: "2"
                    - text: "["
                    - link "19" [ref=e147] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B19-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. As a type of metalloenzyme, hydrogenases contain complex metal clusters and various auxiliary factors which have the potential to be engineered."
                  - generic [ref=e148]:
                    - heading "2.1.1. Metal Clusters of Artificial [Ni-Fe] Hydrogenases" [level=4] [ref=e149]
                    - paragraph [ref=e150]:
                      - text: Since the first crystal structure of [Ni-Fe] hydrogenase (DG) was published by Volbeda et al. at 2.85 Å in 1995 [
                      - link "20" [ref=e151] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B20-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "], there have been increasing numbers of the structures and/or functions of [Ni-Fe] hydrogenases ("
                      - link "Figure 1" [ref=e152] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#molecules-28-05850-f001"
                      - text: A). [Ni-Fe] hydrogenases rely on heterodimeric [Ni-Fe] cofactors to function. Because the synthesis of heterogenous bimetallic cofactors is difficult and their electronic structures are more complex, there is little research on directly modifying the metal co-factors of [Ni-Fe] hydrogenases or integrating artificially synthesized co-factors into [Ni-Fe] hydrogenases. Instead, the focus is mainly on simulating the active site structure of [Ni-Fe] hydrogenases and using the formed simulated complexes as catalysts for H
                      - subscript [ref=e153]: "2"
                      - text: production. For example, there are currently many heterobimetallic transition metal complexes—Ni-Fe [
                      - link "21" [ref=e154] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B21-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "22" [ref=e155] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B22-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "23" [ref=e156] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B23-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "24" [ref=e157] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B24-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "], Ni-Ru ["
                      - link "3" [ref=e158] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B3-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "25" [ref=e159] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B25-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "26" [ref=e160] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B26-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "], and Ni-Mn ["
                      - link "4" [ref=e161] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B4-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "27" [ref=e162] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B27-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "] model complexes—being used as catalysts."
                    - paragraph [ref=e163]:
                      - text: In addition, researchers have attempted to achieve the [Ni-Fe] hydrogenase activity in other proteins. Slater et al. prepared nickel-substituted rubredoxin (NiRd), which has the same four-sulfur-coordinated environment as natural [Ni-Fe] hydrogenase around monovalent nickel [
                      - link "28" [ref=e164] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B28-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "29" [ref=e165] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B29-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. The study found that the recombinant NiRd from"
                      - emphasis [ref=e166]: Desulfovibrio desulfuricans
                      - text: ATCC 27,774 showed high H
                      - subscript [ref=e167]: "2"
                      - text: production activity in the solution [
                      - link "28" [ref=e168] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B28-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Additionally, this NiRd protein had an advantage over natural [Ni-Fe] hydrogenases, since the NiRd protein is completely insensitive to the presence of O"
                      - subscript [ref=e169]: "2"
                      - text: "["
                      - link "29" [ref=e170] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B29-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]."
                  - generic [ref=e171]:
                    - heading "2.1.2. Metal Clusters of Artificial [Fe-Fe] Hydrogenases" [level=4] [ref=e172]
                    - paragraph [ref=e173]:
                      - text: "[Fe-Fe] hydrogenases have the highest efficiency in the process of proton reduction to H"
                      - subscript [ref=e174]: "2"
                      - text: ", with a conversion frequency of up to 10"
                      - superscript [ref=e175]: "4"
                      - text: s
                      - superscript [ref=e176]: −1
                      - text: "["
                      - link "30" [ref=e177] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B30-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Many researchers studied the biomimetic simulation of [Fe-Fe] hydrogenase metal clusters, mainly by simulating the two iron sites of the H cluster ("
                      - link "Figure 1" [ref=e178] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#molecules-28-05850-f001"
                      - text: B), with azadithiolate (ADT) linking the two Fe atoms of [Fe-Fe] hydrogenases [
                      - link "31" [ref=e179] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B31-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]."
                    - paragraph [ref=e180]:
                      - text: The biosynthesis of [2Fe] subclusters in vivo requires the involvement of the specific maturation proteins HydF, HydE, and HydG for hydrogenases. The role of HydF is to transfer the [2Fe] subcluster mimic to the carrier lipid hydrogenase for activation.
                      - emphasis [ref=e181]: E. coli
                      - text: lacks the three maturation proteins. When HydF is expressed heterogeneously in
                      - emphasis [ref=e182]: E. coli
                      - text: ", the [2Fe] subcluster-deficient hydrogenase can be artificially matured to produce functional enzymes with [2Fe] subcluster mimics. Therefore, this artificial [2Fe]"
                      - superscript [ref=e183]: adt
                      - text: maturation can be used to screen for [Fe-Fe] hydrogenases without the expression of the three maturation proteins simultaneously [
                      - link "32" [ref=e184] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B32-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "33" [ref=e185] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B33-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "34" [ref=e186] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B34-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "35" [ref=e187] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B35-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Furthermore, Esselborn et al. found that in the absence of the accessory protein HydF, a mimic synthesized by adding a CO group to the base of the H-cluster can be directly inserted into non-active apo-HYDA1 to form a fully active hydrogenase ["
                      - link "35" [ref=e188] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B35-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Kertess et al. synthesized fully active enzymes using another different method of metal substitution. They replaced the dithiolate ligand with Se to form ADSe and successfully integrated it into apo-HydA1 and apo-CpI in vitro. Compared with wild-type hydrogenases, the selenium-containing enzyme is more biased towards H"
                      - subscript [ref=e189]: "2"
                      - text: production [
                      - link "36" [ref=e190] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B36-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]."
                    - paragraph [ref=e191]:
                      - text: In most cases, even slight variations in co-factors can lead to a significant decrease in the catalytic activity of hydrogenases. Therefore, researchers have attempted to modify the metal co-factors of hydrogenases to gain a deeper understanding of their functional characteristics. They have made many efforts in the semi-synthetic method. An artificial [Ru-Ru] hydrogenase has been obtained by replacing two Fe atoms with the rare metal element ruthenium. It has the advantage of capturing key hydride intermediates [
                      - link "37" [ref=e192] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B37-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Adamska-Venkatesh et al. used -CH"
                      - subscript [ref=e193]: "2"
                      - text: to replace the -NH of the [2Fe] subcluster to form a biomimetic complex [Fe
                      - subscript [ref=e194]: "2"
                      - text: (pdt)(CO)
                      - subscript [ref=e195]: "4"
                      - text: (CN)
                      - subscript [ref=e196]: "2"
                      - text: "]"
                      - superscript [ref=e197]: 2−
                      - text: and then integrated it into the [Fe-Fe] hydrogenase of
                      - emphasis [ref=e198]: Chlamydomonas reinhardtii
                      - text: (CrHydA1). The artificial hydrogenase was found to be stabilized in a state similar to the oxidized state H
                      - subscript [ref=e199]: OX
                      - text: "["
                      - link "38" [ref=e200] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B38-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Sommer et al. also used the same analog synthesis method mentioned above to replace ADT with PDT, resulting in a low-activity enzyme with simplified redox behavior. They also obtained the HydA1-PDSe enzyme with the same redox behavior as HydA1-PDT ["
                      - link "39" [ref=e201] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B39-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]."
                    - paragraph [ref=e202]:
                      - text: Researchers have used various de novo-designed proteins to preserve [2Fe] metal cofactors, attempting to explore the behavior of [Fe-Fe] hydrogenase catalytic cofactors in a simpler environment than natural hydrogenases [
                      - link "40" [ref=e203] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B40-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Some studies have emphasized the necessity of pre-organized Cys ligands in a rigid scaffold ["
                      - link "41" [ref=e204] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B41-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Jones et al. found that the peptide motif CXXC has a dual function of pre-organizing Cys ligands and providing water solubility for the complex ["
                      - link "42" [ref=e205] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B42-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Subsequently, they successfully applied this method to construct functional models by doping (μ-S-Cys)"
                      - subscript [ref=e206]: "2"
                      - text: Fe
                      - subscript [ref=e207]: "2"
                      - text: (CO)
                      - subscript [ref=e208]: "6"
                      - text: into apo-cyt c with the CXXC motif. It was found that this artificial complex can release H
                      - subscript [ref=e209]: "2"
                      - text: through photocatalytic cycles of a ruthenium photosensitizer under mild conditions in aqueous media [
                      - link "43" [ref=e210] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B43-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Subsequent studies on the metal cluster mimics mainly focused on designing more refined peptide structures due to the asymmetric substitution and functional splitting of the [2Fe] site in [Fe-Fe] hydrogenases."
                  - generic [ref=e211]:
                    - heading "2.1.3. Metal Clusters of Artificial [Fe] Hydrogenases" [level=4] [ref=e212]
                    - paragraph [ref=e213]:
                      - text: "[Fe] hydrogenases (Hmd) only exist in methanogenic archaea and contain a mononuclear iron center, with FeGP being its catalytic auxiliary factor. By denaturing the hydrogenase in the presence of 2-mercaptoethanol or acetic acid, the FeGP cofactor can be extracted. Reconstituting the cofactor with apo-[Fe] hydrogenase can rebuild active [Fe] hydrogenase ["
                      - link "44" [ref=e214] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B44-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "45" [ref=e215] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B45-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Therefore, the strong extraction/reconstitution ability of this auxiliary factor is the basis for studying the characteristics of metal cofactors. It paves the way for constructing artificial hydrogenases containing non-natural auxiliary factors."
                    - paragraph [ref=e216]:
                      - text: The Fe ion of [Fe] hydrogenase is coordinated by two cis CO ligands, a cysteine-derived thiol ligand and one bi-dentate 2-acetylpyridine-6-(N-alkyl) thiosemicarbazone ligand [
                      - link "19" [ref=e217] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B19-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "46" [ref=e218] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B46-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Since the structure of the FeGP metal cofactor of [Fe] hydrogenase was determined clearly, many Hmd synthetic models have been reported ("
                      - link "Figure 1" [ref=e219] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#molecules-28-05850-f001"
                      - text: C). In 2015, Shima et al. synthesized two FeGP metal cofactor mimics and inserted them into recombinant apo-[Fe] hydrogenase in
                      - emphasis [ref=e220]: E. coli
                      - text: . The first active semi-synthetic [Fe] hydrogenase was successfully constructed, and it showed a higher turnover rate than most known synthetic catalysts but was found not to activate H
                      - subscript [ref=e221]: "2"
                      - text: "["
                      - link "47" [ref=e222] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B47-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]."
                    - paragraph [ref=e223]:
                      - text: Inspired by Mn-catalyzed hydrogenation reactions, Pan et al. incorporated an Mn(I) model into apo-Hmd and obtained an active [Mn] hydrogenase that can heterolytically cleave H
                      - subscript [ref=e224]: "2"
                      - text: at room temperature [
                      - link "48" [ref=e225] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B48-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. They then developed a series of Mn"
                      - superscript [ref=e226]: I
                      - text: mimics of the active site of [Fe] hydrogenases [
                      - link "49" [ref=e227] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B49-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Compared with semi-synthetic [Fe] hydrogenase, the catalytic activity of semi-synthetic [Mn] hydrogenase is higher. But it should be noted that [Mn] hydrogenase had a stronger forward reaction tendency ["
                      - link "50" [ref=e228] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B50-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Because Mn"
                      - superscript [ref=e229]: I
                      - text: mimics are more stable than Fe
                      - superscript [ref=e230]: II
                      - text: mimics, the synthesis of catalytic [Mn] hydrogenase paved the way for structure–activity studies.
                    - paragraph [ref=e231]:
                      - text: "[Fe] hydrogenase contains only one metal, Fe, and can efficiently perform heterolytic cleavage and H"
                      - superscript [ref=e232]: −
                      - text: transfer in low-H
                      - subscript [ref=e233]: "2"
                      - text: "-concentration aqueous media. Therefore, it can be used as an alternative or supplement to traditional hydrogenation catalysts. However, due to certain issues in enzyme preparation, substrate limitations, and functional activity improvement, the application of this enzyme and its artificial models in practical hydrogenation processes has not been widely explored yet."
                    - 'figure "The metal clusters of native and artificial hydrogenases. (A) (1) Metal cluster structure of native [Ni-Fe] hydrogenases [19]; (B) metal cluster structure of [Fe-Fe] hydrogenases: (2) native metal cluster [19]; (3) ADT [35]; (4) ADSe [36]; (5) [Ru-Ru] hydrogenase [37]; (6) CrHydA1(pdt) [38]; (7) HydA1-PDSe [39]; (C) metal cluster structure of [Fe] hydrogenases: (8) native metal cluster [19]; (9) [47]; (10,11) [48]; (12–15) [49]."                         - text: "]."
                - generic [ref=e270]:
                  - heading "2.2. Metal Clusters of Artificial Hemoprotein" [level=3] [ref=e271]
                  - paragraph [ref=e272]:
                    - text: Hemoprotein is one of the most widely used metalloproteins and plays an important role in oxygen transport and storage, electron transfer, and catalysis in biological systems. Its cofactor is iron porphyrin (heme) (Fe-PIX) (
                    - link "Figure 2" [ref=e273] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#molecules-28-05850-f002"
                    - text: A) [
                    - link "51" [ref=e274] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B51-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. The simplest major cofactor in nature is heme b, also known as Fe protoporphyrin IX (FePPIX), which has three different peripheral substituents composed of four methyls, two vinyls, and two propionic side chains ["
                    - link "52" [ref=e275] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B52-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. Through coordination, hydrogen bonding, hydrophobicity, and electrostatic interactions, heme b binds non-covalently to the heme pocket in proteins. And it is used as a cofactor in myoglobin, hemoglobin, horseradish peroxidase (HRP), cytochrome b5, and cytochrome P450. These proteins are converted to corresponding apolipoproteins after removing heme cofactors under acidic conditions, leaving a cavity as the heme coordination sphere ["
                    - link "53" [ref=e276] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B53-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. The heme cofactor works as a reaction center to provide biochemical functions ranging from O"
                    - subscript [ref=e277]: "2"
                    - text: or NO storage and transport to catalysis and electron transfer. These functions mainly come from the unique arrangement between the cofactor and protein matrix formed by the ferrous heme pocket.
                  - paragraph [ref=e278]:
                    - text: Among these hemoproteins with heme b as a cofactor, myoglobin is a focus of many studies because it is a storage hemoglobin. However, the heme pocket of the myoglobin has been arranged only to stabilize the heme-bound dioxygen, so the structure is not suitable for the activation of small molecules such as H
                    - subscript [ref=e279]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e280]: "2"
                    - text: and O
                    - subscript [ref=e281]: "2"
                    - text: "as well as for binding external substrates. Thus, converting myoglobin into an enzyme-like biocatalyst has important implications. Currently, there are three main methods for designing artificial heme cofactors: (i) the metal substitution of heme; (ii) the modification of peripheral functional groups of porphyrin ligands; and (iii) providing non-natural porphyrin/non-porphyrin cofactor skeletons ["
                    - link "52" [ref=e282] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B52-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "54" [ref=e283] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B54-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]."
                  - generic [ref=e284]:
                    - heading "2.2.1. Metal Substitution of Heme in the Cofactor" [level=4] [ref=e285]
                    - paragraph [ref=e286]:
                      - text: Natural heme enzymes are capable of catalyzing C-H oxidation and halogenation reactions, and Fe-PIX proteins have also been shown to catalyze non-biological reactions, including the addition and insertion of carbene and nitroalkene [
                      - link "55" [ref=e287] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B55-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. However, due to the inherent reactivity of metal ions/cofactors themselves, these reactions are subject to certain limitations. For example, Fe-PIX proteins catalyze the insertion of carbene into strongly reactive N-H bonds and S-H bonds but do not catalyze the insertion into weakly reactive C-H bonds ["
                      - link "55" [ref=e288] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B55-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "56" [ref=e289] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B56-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "57" [ref=e290] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B57-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Dydio et al. discovered that a variant of CYP119 containing iridium-substituted iron could catalyze carbene insertion into C-H bonds with high enantioselectivity ["
                      - link "58" [ref=e291] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B58-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. The method proposed by them for preparing artificial heme proteins containing non-biological metal porphyrins laid the foundation for generating artificial enzymes from the combination of a PIX-protein scaffold and non-natural metal cofactors ["
                      - link "55" [ref=e292] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B55-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. In addition, Wolf et al. produced an artificial metal enzyme using ruthenium porphyrin IX recombinantly, and the results showed that RuMb was an effective N-H insertion catalyst. Compared with natural Mb, RuMb was a more active catalyst in carbene transfer reactions ["
                      - link "59" [ref=e293] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B59-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]."
                    - paragraph [ref=e294]:
                      - text: Compared with transition metals, cobalt is abundant and relatively inexpensive, making cobalt metal substitution important. Generally,
                      - emphasis [ref=e295]: E. coli
                      - text: can produce a new natural CoPPIX cofactor without genetic engineering, evolutionary adaptation, or auxiliary plasmids. Its efficiency in biosynthesis and integration into various heterologously expressed hemoproteins is similar to those of the natural FePPIX cofactor (
                      - link "Figure 2" [ref=e296] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#molecules-28-05850-f002"
                      - text: B) [
                      - link "60" [ref=e297] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B60-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Sommer et al. used Co substitution to produce cobalt myoglobin, which can induce hydrogen production under mild aerobic conditions ["
                      - link "61" [ref=e298] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B61-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. In addition, CoMb can also photo-catalyze the reduction of CO"
                      - subscript [ref=e299]: "2"
                      - text: to CO in the presence of [Ru(bpy)
                      - subscript [ref=e300]: "3"
                      - text: "]"
                      - superscript [ref=e301]: 2+
                      - text: ", with the highest product selectivity among engineered enzymes ["
                      - link "62" [ref=e302] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B62-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Therefore, enzymes with non-natural or artificially introduced metal centers can generate new reactivities, catalyzing unexpected and novel reactions. For example, Shi et al. reported an artificial Mb constructed from zinc protoporphyrin, which exhibited new light-induced DNA cleavage activity ["
                      - link "63" [ref=e303] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B63-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]."
                  - generic [ref=e304]:
                    - heading "2.2.2. Modification of Peripheral Functional Groups of Porphyrin Ligands" [level=4] [ref=e305]
                    - paragraph [ref=e306]:
                      - text: Mb and HRP have the same cofactor, heme b, and both have two His residues at the proximal and distal ends [
                      - link "52" [ref=e307] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B52-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. However, the peroxidase activity of Mb is much lower than that of HRP, because (i) while H"
                      - subscript [ref=e308]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e309]: "2"
                      - text: binds to heme in myoglobin, it is not properly activated to provide peroxidase activity, and (ii) there is no obvious substrate-binding domain in myoglobin [
                      - link "53" [ref=e310] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B53-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. The propionic side chain in myoglobin stabilizes and regulates the heme pocket through charge–charge interactions and/or hydrogen bonding with amino acid residues and to some extent regulates the function of Mb ["
                      - link "51" [ref=e311] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B51-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Various groups attached to the propionic acid side chain can serve as artificial substrate binding domains, helping organic substrates/proteins to enter the vicinity of ferrous heme in Mb, which is the key to the peroxidase activity of Mb. Asp residue can serve as an acidic-basic unit to activate H"
                      - subscript [ref=e312]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e313]: "2"
                      - text: ", so the mutant H64D has an environment similar to HRP ["
                      - link "64" [ref=e314] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B64-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]."
                    - paragraph [ref=e315]:
                      - text: Therefore, based on the above method, Hayashi et al. introduced the H64D mutation and introduced an aromatic group at the end of the propionic acid side chain (
                      - link "Figure 2" [ref=e316] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#molecules-28-05850-f002"
                      - text: C). They used synthetic chemical strategies to connect the substrate binding domain “double-winged cofactor”. The peroxidase activity of the resulting artificial reconstituted Mb was significantly improved, with a k
                      - subscript [ref=e317]: cat
                      - text: /K
                      - subscript [ref=e318]: m
                      - text: only three times lower than that of HRP. However, it was found that this modification caused the k
                      - subscript [ref=e319]: cat
                      - text: value of the mutant Mb to be much lower than that of the mutant containing native heme [
                      - link "64" [ref=e320] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B64-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "65" [ref=e321] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B65-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Therefore, in subsequent studies, they incorporated a “single-winged cofactor” into apo-H64D Mb to reconstitute the protein. As a result, the formed reconstituted Mb was found to have a larger k"
                      - subscript [ref=e322]: cat
                      - text: value, and its peroxidase activity was equivalent to that of HRP. This indicates that the significance of an unmodified propionic acid side chain is to stabilize the orientation of the heme in the heme pocket and form hydrogen bonds [
                      - link "64" [ref=e323] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B64-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. In addition, Sakamoto et al. introduced another molecule into the propionic acid side chain, a peptide heme (br)"
                      - subscript [ref=e324]: "2"
                      - text: ", as an artificial DNA binding site and incorporated it into apo-Mb to obtain Mb(br)"
                      - subscript [ref=e325]: "2"
                      - text: ", which also enhanced peroxidase activity ["
                      - link "66" [ref=e326] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B66-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]."
                    - paragraph [ref=e327]:
                      - text: By modifying the propionic acid side chain of heme b, hemoprotein can be transformed into a protein with new functions. For example, to create a protein that triggers light-induced electron transfer, researchers used modified zinc protoporphyrin recombinantly and introduced four ammonium groups at the end of the two propionic acid side chains [
                      - link "67" [ref=e328] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B67-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Some studies have introduced a flavin into the propionic acid side chains of Mb, which gives the recombinant Mb an electron transfer mechanism similar to that of cytochrome P450. This artificial Mb can activate dioxygen, and it has been successfully converted to an oxygen-activating hemoprotein ["
                      - link "68" [ref=e329] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B68-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]."
                  - generic [ref=e330]:
                    - heading "2.2.3. Providing Non-Natural Porphyrin/Non-Porphyrin Cofactor Scaffolds" [level=4] [ref=e331]
                    - paragraph [ref=e332]:
                      - text: Relative to the natural porphyrin scaffold, the porphyrin-like scaffold has unique characteristics and different structures (
                      - link "Figure 2" [ref=e333] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#molecules-28-05850-f002"
                      - text: D). Therefore, non-natural porphyrin-like scaffold metal complexes have significantly different reactive activities compared to heme [
                      - link "54" [ref=e334] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B54-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Researchers have designed and synthesized various artificial porphyrin analog compounds to change the redox and reactive activity of the metal ion in the porphyrin nucleus in order to achieve the functional modification and improvement of hemoproteins ["
                      - link "52" [ref=e335] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B52-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]."
                    - paragraph [ref=e336]:
                      - text: Corrin is a monoanionic porphyrinoid ligand that lacks one of the four central carbons in the porphyrin framework. Hayashi et al. designed and prepared tetradehydrocorrin and incorporated it into the heme pocket of apo-Mb to form an artificial recombinant Co (TDHC). It can serve as a simple model for the active site of a complex cobalamin-dependent methyltransferase [
                      - link "69" [ref=e337] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B69-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]."
                    - paragraph [ref=e338]:
                      - text: Porphycene is an isomer of porphyrin and a dianionic porphyrinoid used to construct artificial metal proteins. It has lower symmetry than porphyrin [
                      - link "54" [ref=e339] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B54-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "70" [ref=e340] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B70-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Some physical and chemical properties of porphycene are significantly different from those of porphyrin, and it has been found that hemoprotein recombined with metal porphycene exhibits unique properties."
                    - paragraph [ref=e341]:
                      - text: Hayashi et al. designed and produced a novel myoglobin FePc reconstructed with iron porphycene and found that it had a significantly higher oxygen binding affinity and peroxidase activity than natural Mb [
                      - link "71" [ref=e342] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B71-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "72" [ref=e343] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B72-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "73" [ref=e344] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B73-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. In 2007, they also incorporated iron porphycene into the heme pocket of HRP and found that this protein had higher reactivity towards the oxidation of thioanisole ["
                      - link "74" [ref=e345] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B74-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Natural myoglobin does not have hydroxylation activity, but some studies have prepared an Mn porphycene myoglobin (rMb (MnPc)) and incorporated it into apo-Mb, finding that it significantly enhanced the catalytic activity of myoglobin towards the C(sp3)-H hydroxylation of substrates ["
                      - link "75" [ref=e346] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B75-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "76" [ref=e347] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B76-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]."
                    - paragraph [ref=e348]:
                      - text: Corrole is a trianionic porphyrin ligand that lacks a carbon atom between two of the four pyrrole units compared to the porphyrin scaffold [
                      - link "52" [ref=e349] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B52-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Matsuo et al. designed and synthesized an iron corrole complex (FeCor) and incorporated it into apo-Mb and apo-HRP. It was found that the recombinant myoglobin exhibited significantly enhanced peroxidase activity ["
                      - link "77" [ref=e350] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B77-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]."
                    - paragraph [ref=e351]:
                      - text: Non-porphyrin cofactor ligands have also been widely studied as cofactors for artificial metalloenzymes (
                      - link "Figure 2" [ref=e352] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#molecules-28-05850-f002"
                      - text: D), leading to the development of some recombinant proteins with new functions. Ueno et al. prepared a hybrid metalloprotein using apo-Mb and a Schiff-base complex, which promoted the rapid consumption of NADH and O
                      - subscript [ref=e353]: "2"
                      - text: in heme oxygenase [
                      - link "78" [ref=e354] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B78-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Carey et al. used a new dual-covalent anchoring binding strategy to incorporate Mn(salen) into apo-Mb. It was found that this method significantly improved the enantioselectivity of the semisynthetic enzyme and increased the rate of the sulfoxidation of thioanisole ["
                      - link "79" [ref=e355] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B79-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]. Bacchi et al. created an artificial recombinant protein with hydrogenation enzyme properties by incorporating two cobaloximes into apo-SwMb, which catalyzed H"
                      - subscript [ref=e356]: "2"
                      - text: evolution at low overpotentials [
                      - link "80" [ref=e357] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B80-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]."
                    - 'figure "The metal clusters of native and artificial hemoprotein. (A) (16) heme (Fe-PIX) [51]; (B) metal substitution of heme: (17) CoPPIX [60,62]; (18) ZnPP [63]; (C) modify the propionic side chain: (19) double-winged cofactor [64,65]; (20) single-winged cofactor [64]; (21) heme(br)2 [66]; (22) [67]; (23) [68]; (D) non-natural porphyrin scaffolds: (24) corrin: Co(TDHC) [69]; (25) porphycene: [71,72,73]; (26) porphycene: MnPc [75,76]; (27) corrole: FeCor [77]; (D) non-porphyrin scaffolds: (28) [78]; (29) Mn(salen) [79]; (30,31) [80]."                           - img "Figure 2" [ref=e362]

                        - text: ) [
                        - link "68" [ref=e388] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B68-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]; ("
                        - strong [ref=e389]: D
                        - text: ") non-natural porphyrin scaffolds: ("
                        - strong [ref=e390]: "24"
                        - text: ") corrin: Co(TDHC) ["
                        - link "69" [ref=e391] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B69-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]; ("
                        - strong [ref=e392]: "25"
                        - text: ") porphycene: ["
                        - link "71" [ref=e393] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B71-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: ","
                        - link "72" [ref=e394] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B72-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: ","
                        - link "73" [ref=e395] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B73-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]; ("
                        - strong [ref=e396]: "26"
                        - text: ") porphycene: MnPc ["
                        - link "75" [ref=e397] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B75-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: ","
                        - link "76" [ref=e398] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B76-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]; ("
                        - strong [ref=e399]: "27"
                        - text: ") corrole: FeCor ["
                        - link "77" [ref=e400] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B77-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]; ("
                        - strong [ref=e401]: D
                        - text: ") non-porphyrin scaffolds: ("
                        - strong [ref=e402]: "28"
                        - text: ) [
                        - link "78" [ref=e403] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B78-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]; ("
                        - strong [ref=e404]: "29"
                        - text: ) Mn(salen) [
                        - link "79" [ref=e405] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B79-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]; ("
                        - strong [ref=e406]: "30"
                        - text: ","
                        - strong [ref=e407]: "31"
                        - text: ) [
                        - link "80" [ref=e408] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B80-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]."
                - generic [ref=e409]:
                  - heading "2.3. Metal Clusters of the Artificial Photosynthesis System" [level=3] [ref=e410]
                  - paragraph [ref=e411]:
                    - text: "In nature, solar energy is utilized and stored through photosynthesis including both light and dark reactions in two photosystems: Photosystem I and II (PSI and PSII). Green plants, algae, and cyanobacteria convert solar energy into protons, electrons, and O"
                    - subscript [ref=e412]: "2"
                    - text: through water oxidation, providing protons and electrons for the second half of the reaction (H
                    - subscript [ref=e413]: "2"
                    - text: production) of water splitting, and H
                    - subscript [ref=e414]: "2"
                    - text: can be used as a new energy source [
                    - link "81" [ref=e415] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B81-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. The water oxidation process is catalyzed by PSII."
                  - table [ref=e416]:
                    - rowgroup [ref=e417]:
                      - row "2H2O → O2 + 4H+ + 4e−" [ref=e418]:
                        - cell "2H2O → O2 + 4H+ + 4e−" [ref=e419]:
                          - generic [ref=e420]:
                            - text: 2H
                            - subscript [ref=e421]: "2"
                            - text: O → O
                            - subscript [ref=e422]: "2"
                            - text: + 4H
                            - superscript [ref=e423]: +
                            - text: + 4e
                            - superscript [ref=e424]: −
                  - paragraph [ref=e425]:
                    - text: PSII is a large protein complex composed of more than 20 subunits, with a molecular weight of approximately 350 kDa [
                    - link "82" [ref=e426] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B82-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "83" [ref=e427] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B83-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. Its essential elements are (i) a strongly oxidizing multichlorophyll complex termed P680; (ii) a redox-active tyrosine termed Y"
                    - subscript [ref=e428]: Z
                    - text: ; (iii) a bound plastoquinone electron acceptor; and (iv) a metalloprotein containing a manganese, calcium cluster called the oxygen-evolving center (OEC) [
                    - link "84" [ref=e429] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B84-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "85" [ref=e430] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B85-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. In 2011, Umena et al. reported the crystal structure of PSII at a resolution of 1.9 Å and identified the metal cluster in the OEC as Mn"
                    - subscript [ref=e431]: "4"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e432]: "5"
                    - text: "-Ca(H"
                    - subscript [ref=e433]: "2"
                    - text: O)
                    - subscript [ref=e434]: "4"
                    - text: "["
                    - link "83" [ref=e435] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B83-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. The OEC is capable of water oxidation at a low overpotential (≈0.18 V) and a high rate (≈500 s"
                    - superscript [ref=e436]: −1
                    - text: ) [
                    - link "85" [ref=e437] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B85-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. So, the rate of O"
                    - subscript [ref=e438]: "2"
                    - text: production by the oxygen-evolving complex (OEC) is comparatively high.
                  - paragraph [ref=e439]:
                    - text: Currently, studies are mainly focused on synthesizing relatively simple metal analogs that combine catalytic activity with chemical stability to achieve the catalytic efficiency of the OEC. As a result, many molecular complexes have been developed to catalyze water oxidation. Early research on water oxidation catalysts focused mainly on complexes based on Ru [
                    - link "86" [ref=e440] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B86-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "87" [ref=e441] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B87-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "88" [ref=e442] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B88-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "] and Ir ["
                    - link "89" [ref=e443] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B89-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "90" [ref=e444] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B90-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "91" [ref=e445] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B91-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "], while current research has shifted its focus to non-noble metal catalysts based on abundant metals on Earth such as Fe ["
                    - link "92" [ref=e446] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B92-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "93" [ref=e447] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B93-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "], Co ["
                    - link "94" [ref=e448] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B94-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "95" [ref=e449] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B95-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "], Ni ["
                    - link "96" [ref=e450] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B96-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "97" [ref=e451] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B97-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "98" [ref=e452] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B98-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "], and Cu ["
                    - link "99" [ref=e453] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B99-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "100" [ref=e454] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B100-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "101" [ref=e455] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B101-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. These studies have deepened our understanding of the mechanism of water oxidation and resulted in a series of catalytic agents with activity in water oxidation. These artificially synthesized water oxidation catalysts can provide possibilities for sustainable artificial photosynthesis."
                  - paragraph [ref=e456]:
                    - text: The complexity of PSII makes its direct application in fuel production impractical, but the de novo design of proteins provides a new approach to artificial photosynthesis. Conlan et al. used a modified form of bacterioferritin (BFR) from
                    - emphasis [ref=e457]: E. coli
                    - text: as a protein scaffold to propose an artificial PSII model [
                    - link "102" [ref=e458] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B102-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. The reconstruction of the photosynthetic reaction center offers a way to directly redesign photosynthesis for human needs. Researchers have developed a single-reaction-center photosynthetic system that supports water oxidation and proton reduction. This model system achieves long-lived, light-driven charge separation and contains many elements from natural photosynthetic reaction centers ["
                    - link "103" [ref=e459] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B103-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. In addition, researchers have used the template Df2t homodimer protein structure to design three different four-helix bundle proteins, P0, P1, and P2, de novo, each containing one, two, or three dinuclear Mn centers. These artificial manganese proteins are capable of transferring electrons to bacterial reaction centers, providing an opportunity to study the oxidation-reduction properties of the dinuclear manganese cofactor ["
                    - link "104" [ref=e460] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B104-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]."
              - generic [ref=e461]:
                - heading "3. Coenzyme Analogs" [level=2] [ref=e462]: 3. Coenzyme Analogs
                - paragraph [ref=e463]:
                  - text: Oxidoreductases are the largest group of enzymes reported to date, and their catalytic reactions typically require coenzymes to transfer electrons, hydrogen, oxygen, or other small-molecule intermediates [
                  - link "105" [ref=e464] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B105-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. Typical coenzymes include nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide (NAD), nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate (NADP), ubiquinone (CoQ), and flavin mononucleotide (FMN/FAD), among which NAD, dependent for 80% of oxidoreductases, is the most popular. NAD and its reduced form NADH are essential electron carriers for many redox reactions and substrates for some biological reactions, playing important roles in cellular metabolism ["
                  - link "106" [ref=e465] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B106-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "107" [ref=e466] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B107-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. However, natural coenzymes like NAD are unstable, expensive to use in vitro, and difficult to control in vivo ["
                  - link "108" [ref=e467] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B108-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], which limits their application in large-scale synthesis and intrigues researchers to develop non-natural coenzymes. Non-natural coenzymes (mNADs) have industrial value in reducing feedstock costs because mNADs are generally simpler to synthesize and have greater stability than natural coenzymes ["
                  - link "109" [ref=e468] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B109-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], allowing access to new chemicals with altered redox potentials ["
                  - link "110" [ref=e469] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B110-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "] and enabling the specific delivery of electrons ["
                  - link "9" [ref=e470] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B9-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. At the same time, mNADs are valuable molecular tools for detecting, monitoring, structurally studying, and regulating the activity of NAD-related enzymes and biological processes. A biocompatible emissive mNAD has been designed to realize the real-time visualization of cofactor-dependent processes through fluorescence spectroscopy ["
                  - link "111" [ref=e471] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B111-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]."
                - paragraph [ref=e472]:
                  - text: The structure of natural coenzymes is divided into the nicotinamide mononucleotide (NMN) moiety responsible for transferring hydrogen and electrons and the adenosine phosphate (AMP) moiety responsible for anchoring the coenzyme and enzyme interaction. Some mNADs were obtained by modifying or replacing the carboxamide [
                  - link "112" [ref=e473] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B112-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], the adenine base ["
                  - link "113" [ref=e474] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B113-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "114" [ref=e475] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B114-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "115" [ref=e476] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B115-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], and the nicotinamide ribose ["
                  - link "116" [ref=e477] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B116-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "] of NAD with alternative functional groups, as shown in"
                  - link "Figure 3" [ref=e478] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#molecules-28-05850-f003"
                  - text: . Since the redox chemistry of NAD occurs in the NMN moiety, the synthesis of mNADs mainly involves modifying the AMP moiety of natural coenzymes [
                  - link "117" [ref=e479] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B117-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "] and can be classified into two categories. One is semi-synthetic biomimetic coenzymes that are similar to natural nicotinamide coenzymes in structure, usually in truncated forms of natural coenzymes (such as NMN) or only modifying and replacing some groups of natural nicotinamide coenzymes ["
                  - link "9" [ref=e480] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B9-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "10" [ref=e481] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B10-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. The other category is fully synthetic biomimetic coenzymes ["
                  - link "118" [ref=e482] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B118-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], which are typically small in size (e.g., BNA) and retain only the niacinamide group responsible for electron transfer."
                - 'figure "Chemical structures of NAD and mNADs. Full names of the mNADs: MNA, 1-methyl-1,4-dihydropyridine-3-carboxamide; BNA, 1-benzyl-1,4-dihydronicotinamide; P2NA, 1-phenethyl-1,4-dihydropyridine-3-carboxamide; NMN, Nicotinamide mononucleotide; NCD, Nicotinamide cytosine dinucleotide; NFCD, Nicotinamide flucytosine dinucleotide." [ref=e483]':
                  - heading "Figure 3." [level=3] [ref=e484]
                  - paragraph [ref=e485]:
                    - link "Figure 3 🔍" [ref=e486] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=10421094_molecules-28-05850-g003.jpg
                      - img "Figure 3" [ref=e487]
                      - text: 🔍
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e489] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/molecules-28-05850-f003/
                  - paragraph [ref=e491]: "Chemical structures of NAD and mNADs. Full names of the mNADs: MNA, 1-methyl-1,4-dihydropyridine-3-carboxamide; BNA, 1-benzyl-1,4-dihydronicotinamide; P2NA, 1-phenethyl-1,4-dihydropyridine-3-carboxamide; NMN, Nicotinamide mononucleotide; NCD, Nicotinamide cytosine dinucleotide; NFCD, Nicotinamide flucytosine dinucleotide."
                - paragraph [ref=e492]:
                  - text: A series of biologically active NAD analogs have been reported, such as non-natural coenzyme nicotinamide cytosine dinucleotide (NCD), with only one base difference from NAD [
                  - link "119" [ref=e493] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B119-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. Most of these mNADs were chemically or chemoenzymatically synthesized, including N"
                  - superscript [ref=e494]: TZ
                  - text: AD containing thiazolo [4, 3-d] pyrimidine moiety [
                  - link "111" [ref=e495] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B111-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "] and 4′-thioribose NAD ["
                  - link "120" [ref=e496] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B120-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. It is worth noting that, except for MNA, BNA, P2NA, and other stable compounds that could be easily synthesized by treating nicotinamide with alkyl halides ["
                  - link "121" [ref=e497] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B121-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], most of the chemical synthesis methods were complicated with low product yields. For example, NCD prepared by the chemical synthesis of NMN and CMP ("
                  - link "Figure 4" [ref=e498] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#molecules-28-05850-f004"
                  - text: A) upon tedious post-processing could yield only about 100 mg per batch [
                  - link "6" [ref=e499] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B6-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. To facilitate the mNADs-linked redox chemistry in vivo, it is essential to achieve the biosynthesis of mNADs. In 2022, Zhao and his colleagues developed a simple and rapid method ("
                  - link "Figure 4" [ref=e500] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#molecules-28-05850-f004"
                  - text: B) for achieving the gram-scale biocatalytic preparation of NCD [
                  - link "6" [ref=e501] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B6-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. First, they generated an NCD synthetase (NcdS-1) by reprogramming the substrate binding pocket of nicotinic acid mononucleotide (NaMN) adenylyltransferase, enabling it to be favorable to cytidine triphosphate (CTP) and NMN, respectively, rather than their conventional substrates adenine triphosphate (ATP) and NaMN ["
                  - link "122" [ref=e502] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B122-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "123" [ref=e503] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B123-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. With the help of Ncds-1, the efficient catalysis of NMN and CTP was achieved. Subsequently, the inorganic pyrophosphatase (PPase) from"
                  - emphasis [ref=e504]: Escherichia coli
                  - text: was used to optimize the reaction composition and achieved a near-quantitative conversion of substrates. By centrifugation, ultrafiltration, concentration, precipitation, and ion exchange chromatography, they obtained a 4.19 g NCD product with 96% absolute purity from a production scale of 300 mL and 30 mM NCD, ultimately.
                - figure "Reactions related to mNADs synthesis. (A) Chemical synthesis of NCD; (B) Biosynthesis of NCD using CTP and NMN; (C) Biosynthesis of NMN using glucose and NAM." [ref=e505]:
                  - heading "Figure 4." [level=3] [ref=e506]
                  - paragraph [ref=e507]:
                    - link "Figure 4 🔍" [ref=e508] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=10421094_molecules-28-05850-g004.jpg
                      - img "Figure 4" [ref=e509]
                      - text: 🔍
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e511] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/molecules-28-05850-f004/
                  - paragraph [ref=e513]:
                    - text: Reactions related to mNADs synthesis. (
                    - strong [ref=e514]: A
                    - text: ) Chemical synthesis of NCD; (
                    - strong [ref=e515]: B
                    - text: ) Biosynthesis of NCD using CTP and NMN; (
                    - strong [ref=e516]: C
                    - text: ) Biosynthesis of NMN using glucose and NAM.
                - paragraph [ref=e517]:
                  - text: In addition, the synthetic precursor of NCD, NMN, has also been biosynthesized in
                  - emphasis [ref=e518]: E. coli
                  - text: . Yu et al. [
                  - link "7" [ref=e519] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B7-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "] adopted synthetic biology strategies to design strains with improved performance, including screening for exogenous Nampt enzymes, enhancing the availability of precursor phosphoribosyl pyrophosphate (PRPP) and ATP, and exploring homologs of natural nicotinamide (NAM) transporters. Combined with the process optimization of whole-cell biocatalyst reactions, a simple biotransformation process was developed for the synthesis of NMN from an inexpensive substrate NAM and glucose, and an NMN titer of 496.2 mg L"
                  - superscript [ref=e520]: −1
                  - text: was obtained. During the same period, a study reported the synthesis of the highest yield of NMN to date, which produced 6.79 g L
                  - superscript [ref=e521]: −1
                  - text: NMN extracellularly from glucose and NAM by an engineered
                  - emphasis [ref=e522]: E. coli
                  - text: BL21(DE3) strain with a plasmid-based protein expression system (
                  - link "Figure 4" [ref=e523] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#molecules-28-05850-f004"
                  - text: C) [
                  - link "8" [ref=e524] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B8-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. They identified two active functional transporters (NiaP and PnuC) and a highly active key enzyme (Nampt) that allowed for NAM uptake, the efficient conversion of PRPP (provided by glucose) and NAM to NMN, as well as extracellular NMN excretion. The enhancement of the PRPP biosynthetic pathway and the optimization of individual gene expression resulted in much higher NMN production than previously reported. The biosynthesis methods described above are expected to be used for the industrial production of low-cost, high-quality mNADs by making use of microorganisms."
                - paragraph [ref=e525]:
                  - text: Most wild-type enzymes have poor utilization of mNADs, that is, they still prefer NAD. However, many flavor enzymes, such as enoate reductases, nitroreductases, and para-hydroxybenzoate hydroxylase, exhibit promising activity [
                  - link "9" [ref=e526] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B9-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "124" [ref=e527] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B124-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "125" [ref=e528] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B125-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. To expand the regulatory capacity of redox metabolism, efforts have been made to create mNADs-dependent oxidoreductases, including malic enzyme ["
                  - link "119" [ref=e529] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B119-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "126" [ref=e530] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B126-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], phosphite dehydrogenase ["
                  - link "115" [ref=e531] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B115-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "127" [ref=e532] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B127-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], formate dehydrogenase ["
                  - link "128" [ref=e533] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B128-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], glucose dehydrogenase ["
                  - link "9" [ref=e534] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B9-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], d-lactate dehydrogenase ["
                  - link "113" [ref=e535] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B113-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "119" [ref=e536] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B119-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], methanol dehydrogenase ["
                  - link "129" [ref=e537] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B129-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], formaldehyde dehydrogenase ["
                  - link "11" [ref=e538] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B11-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], and so on. In general, the coenzyme preference modification of oxidoreductases can be achieved by classical protein engineering methods: random mutagenesis, semi-rational design, and rational design. Since most NAD(P)-dependent oxidoreductases have a highly conserved coenzyme-binding Rossmann fold sequence ["
                  - link "130" [ref=e539] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B130-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], semi-rational design and rational design are more widely used in oxidoreductase coenzyme engineering. Sieber et al. ["
                  - link "116" [ref=e540] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B116-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "] carried out a rational design on glucose dehydrogenase ("
                  - emphasis [ref=e541]: Ss
                  - text: GDH) derived from
                  - emphasis [ref=e542]: Sulfolobus solfataricus
                  - text: and screened a double-mutant Ile192Thr/Val306Ile through the site-specific saturation mutagenesis of nine amino acid residues near the coenzyme pocket. The mutant showed a 10-fold increase in enzymatic activity towards the non-natural coenzyme P2NA compared with the wild-type enzyme and was successfully applied in 2-methylbutanal production. Li et al. [
                  - link "9" [ref=e543] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B9-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "] achieved the maximum specific conversion (10"
                  - superscript [ref=e544]: "7"
                  - text: "-fold) of glucose dehydrogenase ("
                  - emphasis [ref=e545]: Bs
                  - text: GDH) from
                  - emphasis [ref=e546]: Bacillus subtilis
                  - text: to a non-natural coenzyme employing computer-aided design. The engineered mutant strictly relied on NMN and was successfully used for the enzymatic reaction in vitro. Similarly, Huang et al. successfully improved the activity of
                  - emphasis [ref=e547]: Tm
                  - text: 6PGDH to NMN by utilizing high-throughput screening methods. The optimal mutant showed a 50-fold higher catalytic efficiency towards NMN than the wild-type enzyme, whose activity towards NMN was comparable to that of the wild-type enzyme towards the natural coenzyme NADP [
                  - link "10" [ref=e548] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B10-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], and this could be used for in vitro synthetic biology."
                - paragraph [ref=e549]:
                  - text: The research team led by Professor Zongbao Kent Zhao has made significant contributions to the application of mNADs in biological orthogonal systems. They used a semi-rational approach to evolve the phosphite dehydrogenase (Pdh) from
