                  - emphasis [ref=e550]: Ralstonia
                  - text: sp. strain 4506 to obtain a mutant I151R/P176R/M207A that could utilize NCD as a coenzyme. The enzymatic activity of this mutant towards NCD and that of the wild-type enzyme towards NAD were found to be of the same order of magnitude. The team then summarized the general rules for modifying dehydrogenase preferences through the crystal structure and mechanism analysis. The basic principle is to shrink the volume of the coenzyme binding pocket by introducing residues with large side chains, which are unfavorable for NAD binding, and to achieve a preference for coenzymes with a reduced size [
                  - link "115" [ref=e551] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B115-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. It is of great significance for constructing engineered enzymes to utilize mNADs efficiently. Using the same method, the team obtained various NCD-preferred oxidoreductase mutants and constructed corresponding orthogonal redox catalytic systems and phosphite-driven organic acid synthesis systems. By coupling the engineered formate dehydrogenase (FDH*) ["
                  - link "128" [ref=e552] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B128-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "] with malic enzyme (ME*) and"
                  - subscript [ref=e553]: D
                  - text: "-lactate dehydrogenase (DLDH*) ["
                  - link "119" [ref=e554] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B119-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], they constructed a formate-driven, mNADs-mediated malate biosynthesis and oxidative decarboxylation system in microbial cells ("
                  - link "Figure 5" [ref=e555] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#molecules-28-05850-f005"
                  - text: ). As a result, NCD has become an artificial coenzyme successfully biosynthesized for use in intracellular orthogonal redox reactions. This research is of significant reference value for the artificial design of metabolic pathways and the selective regulation of cellular material energy metabolism.
                - figure "Non-natural coenzyme (mNADs) orthogonal system of NCD and NFCD with mutated formate dehydrogenase (FDH*), malic enzyme (ME*), and d-lactate dehydrogenase (DLDH*)." [ref=e556]:
                  - heading "Figure 5." [level=3] [ref=e557]
                  - paragraph [ref=e558]:
                    - link "Figure 5 🔍" [ref=e559] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=10421094_molecules-28-05850-g005.jpg
                      - img "Figure 5" [ref=e560]
                      - text: 🔍
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e562] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/molecules-28-05850-f005/
                  - paragraph [ref=e564]: Non-natural coenzyme (mNADs) orthogonal system of NCD and NFCD with mutated formate dehydrogenase (FDH*), malic enzyme (ME*), and d-lactate dehydrogenase (DLDH*).
                - paragraph [ref=e565]:
                  - text: It is worth mentioning that to further increase the structural diversity and biocompatibility of mNADs, Zhao’s team also synthesized 14 proteogenic amino acids-based NAD analogs through the Zincke reaction and characterized their physicochemical properties such as spectroscopy and redox potential [
                  - link "131" [ref=e566] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B131-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]."
                  - link "Figure 6" [ref=e567] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#molecules-28-05850-f006"
                  - text: showed mNADs with an isolation yield of 90% or above. Several representative mNADs were tested with the bifunctional fatty acid hydroxylase P450 BM3-R966D/W1046S [
                  - link "132" [ref=e568] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B132-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], which was known to have relaxed coenzyme specificity, to explore whether l-AmiNAs could serve as oxidoreductase coenzymes. The preliminary screening results indicated that a few reduced analogs (including l-MetNAH) could effectively promote the fatty acid hydroxylation of the mutant cytochrome P450 enzyme as a coenzyme. However, the role of l-AmiNAHs as a redox enzyme coenzyme was still significantly reduced compared to NADH. Future consideration could be given to designing coenzyme binding pockets of redox enzymes to favor l-AmiNAHs."
                - figure "Synthesis of proteogenic amino acid-based mNADs." [ref=e569]:
                  - heading "Figure 6." [level=3] [ref=e570]
                  - paragraph [ref=e571]:
                    - link "Figure 6 🔍" [ref=e572] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=10421094_molecules-28-05850-g006.jpg
                      - img "Figure 6" [ref=e573]
                      - text: 🔍
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e575] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/molecules-28-05850-f006/
                  - paragraph [ref=e577]: Synthesis of proteogenic amino acid-based mNADs.
                - paragraph [ref=e578]: In summary, research on the non-natural coenzymes preference of oxidoreductases is still a relatively new field, and the construction of an efficient non-natural coenzymes regeneration system is also in the primary stage. In addition to engineering enzymes to favor mNADs, designing other specific and efficient cofactors for specific enzymes is also an effective approach. In the next section, we will discuss the design of cofactors in detail by taking P450 enzymes as an example.
              - generic [ref=e579]:
                - heading "4. Designer Cofactors" [level=2] [ref=e580]: 4. Designer Cofactors
                - paragraph [ref=e581]:
                  - text: Cytochrome P450 enzymes (CYPs or P450s), catalyzing the monooxygenation of various substrates, including aliphatic and aromatic compounds, alkenes, and heteroatoms, are promising versatile oxidative biocatalysts [
                  - link "133" [ref=e582] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B133-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "134" [ref=e583] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B134-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "135" [ref=e584] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B135-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. Despite their impressive ability to oxidize inert C-H bonds in a regio- and stereoselective manner, the preparative-scale applications of P450s in vitro are limited due to their dependence on the coenzyme NAD(P)H and the complex electron transport system ["
                  - link "136" [ref=e585] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B136-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. Additionally, intrinsic drawbacks such as the narrow scope of useful nonnative substrates, poor stability, and low catalytic rates also limit the practical use of P450s. Protein engineering may help to overcome some of these issues; on the other hand, designer cofactors have been developed to create new catalytic capabilities of P450s and facilitate their further industrial utilization as biocatalysts in vitro ["
                  - link "12" [ref=e586] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B12-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]."
                - paragraph [ref=e587]:
                  - text: Designer cofactors can be classified into several categories, including substrate anchoring groups, decoy molecules, and dual-functional small molecules (DFSMs). Substrate anchoring groups improve the substrate scope and reaction selectivity by engineering the substrate, using the concept of docking and protecting groups in bio-hydroxylation [
                  - link "137" [ref=e588] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B137-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. Decoy molecules with similar structures to the native substrate of P450s, particularly the moieties that are responsible for substrate binding, are used to reshape the active site of the enzymes to accommodate another non-native substrate ["
                  - link "138" [ref=e589] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B138-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "139" [ref=e590] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B139-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. On the other hand, DFSMs not only reform the active site like decoy molecules but also directly participate in the catalytic process, creating new catalytic capabilities towards non-native substrates ["
                  - link "140" [ref=e591] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B140-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]."
                - paragraph [ref=e592]:
                  - text: As is well known, there are two pathways for activating P450s, namely, the NAD(P)H-dependent and the peroxide shunt pathway (
                  - link "Figure 7" [ref=e593] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#molecules-28-05850-f007"
                  - text: A) [
                  - link "141" [ref=e594] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B141-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. Most native P450s adopt the NAD(P)H-dependent pathway and exhibit poor reactivity in the presence of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e595]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e596]: "2"
                  - text: . Therefore, shifting P450s from the NAD(P)H-dependent pathway to the H
                  - subscript [ref=e597]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e598]: "2"
                  - text: "-dependent pathway could be an attractive approach for their practical utilization. Recently, with the assistance of designer cofactor DFSMs, a unique strategy for an H"
                  - subscript [ref=e599]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e600]: "2"
                  - text: "-driven P450BM3 system was developed by Cong and coworkers ["
                  - link "142" [ref=e601] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B142-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. The DFSM comprises three parts, an acyl amino acid group responsible for binding to the enzyme as an anchoring group, an imidazolyl group serving as a general acid-base catalyst in the activation of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e602]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e603]: "2"
                  - text: ", and a short fatty acid that connects them ("
                  - link "Figure 7" [ref=e604] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#molecules-28-05850-f007"
                  - text: C) [
                  - link "142" [ref=e605] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B142-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. Computational investigations reveal that H"
                  - subscript [ref=e606]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e607]: "2"
                  - text: activation by P450BM3 is highly dependent on the DFSM. In the absence of DFSM, the enzyme prefers homolytic O-O cleavage to form compound I (Cpd I), while in the presence of the DFSM, a proton channel formed between the imidazolyl group of the DFSM and the proximal H of H
                  - subscript [ref=e608]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e609]: "2"
                  - text: ", enabling a heterolytic O-O cleavage and Cpd I formation that is greatly favored over the homolysis mechanism ("
                  - link "Figure 7" [ref=e610] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#molecules-28-05850-f007"
                  - text: B) [
                  - link "143" [ref=e611] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B143-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]."
                - figure "The catalytic cycle of cytochrome P450 monooxygenase and the peroxide-shunt pathway (A) and P450BM3-H2O2 system assisted by the DFSM (B) [143], and the representative structures of the dual-functional small molecules (DFSMs) (C) [142]." [ref=e612]:
                  - heading "Figure 7." [level=3] [ref=e613]
                  - paragraph [ref=e614]:
                    - link "Figure 7 🔍" [ref=e615] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=10421094_molecules-28-05850-g007.jpg
                      - img "Figure 7" [ref=e616]
                      - text: 🔍
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e618] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/molecules-28-05850-f007/
                  - paragraph [ref=e620]:
                    - text: The catalytic cycle of cytochrome P450 monooxygenase and the peroxide-shunt pathway (
                    - strong [ref=e621]: A
                    - text: ) and P450BM3-H
                    - subscript [ref=e622]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e623]: "2"
                    - text: system assisted by the DFSM (
                    - strong [ref=e624]: B
                    - text: ) [
                    - link "143" [ref=e625] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B143-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "], and the representative structures of the dual-functional small molecules (DFSMs) ("
                    - strong [ref=e626]: C
                    - text: ) [
                    - link "142" [ref=e627] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B142-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]."
                - paragraph [ref=e628]:
                  - text: This DFSM-facilitated P450-H
                  - subscript [ref=e629]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e630]: "2"
                  - text: system has been developed into versatile biocatalysts for many non-native substrates, showing the best peroxygenase activity for the epoxidation of styrene, the sulfoxidation of thioanisole, and the hydroxylation of ethylbenzene among the previously reported P450-H
                  - subscript [ref=e631]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e632]: "2"
                  - text: systems [
                  - link "142" [ref=e633] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B142-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], as well as the selective hydroxylation of naphthalene ["
                  - link "144" [ref=e634] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B144-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. Moreover, this H"
                  - subscript [ref=e635]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e636]: "2"
                  - text: "-driven P450BM3 system can hydroxylate small alkanes with high regioselectivity, and its turnover number (TON) is comparable to that of the fungal H"
                  - subscript [ref=e637]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e638]: "2"
                  - text: "-dependent natural alkane hydroxylase AaeUPO ["
                  - link "145" [ref=e639] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B145-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "146" [ref=e640] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B146-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. The product formation rates are also similar to or better than those of evolved/engineered NADPH-dependent P450 systems ["
                  - link "146" [ref=e641] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B146-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "147" [ref=e642] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B147-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "148" [ref=e643] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B148-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. The regioselective"
                  - emphasis [ref=e644]: O
                  - text: "-demethylation of various aromatic ethers has been achieved by this DFSM-facilitated peroxygenase system with several simple mutants in the enzyme ["
                  - link "149" [ref=e645] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B149-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. In addition to these high regioselective reactions, this system has also been developed into a highly enantioselective system, such as the epoxidation of styrene and its derivatives"
                  - emphasis [ref=e646]: o
                  - text: "-,"
                  - emphasis [ref=e647]: m
                  - text: "-,"
                  - emphasis [ref=e648]: p
                  - text: "-chlorostyrenes, and fluorostyrenes, with the ("
                  - emphasis [ref=e649]: R
                  - text: )-enantiomeric excess (e.e.) of the products reaching up to 99% [
                  - link "150" [ref=e650] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B150-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. An even more exciting advancement in this design cofactor system is that the DFSM-facilitated system enables access to over half of all possible hydroxylated products from each given alkylbenzenes substrate, with excellent regioselectivity (up to 99%) and enantioselectivity (up to 99% e.e.) and high total TON (up to 80963) ["
                  - link "151" [ref=e651] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B151-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. These results indicate that the synergistic use of an exogenous DFSM and protein engineering constitutes an efficient strategy for controlling the regio- and enantioselectivity of P450BM3 for non-native substrates."
                - paragraph [ref=e652]:
                  - text: In addition to its peroxygenation activity, this DFSM-facilitated P450-H
                  - subscript [ref=e653]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e654]: "2"
                  - text: system displays peroxidase activity and is reinforced towards various classic one-electron oxidation substrates through the combination of site-directed mutations on redox-sensitive residues [
                  - link "152" [ref=e655] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B152-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. It is worth noting that this modified P450 peroxidase system can also catalyze similar reactions by using nitrite as a nitrating agent. The nitration of multiple phenol and aniline compounds results in moderate-to-high total TONs of"
                  - emphasis [ref=e656]: ortho
                  - text: "- and"
                  - emphasis [ref=e657]: para
                  - text: "-nitration products. Furthermore, besides the direct aromatic nitration caused by P450 variants through using nitrite as a nitrating agent, this DFSM-facilitated P450 peroxidase system can also catalyze the nitration of the vinyl group of styrene and its derivatives ["
                  - link "153" [ref=e658] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B153-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]."
                - paragraph [ref=e659]:
                  - text: In general, the designer cofactor DFSM is successful in shifting the NADH-dependent P450BM3 into its peroxygenase or peroxidase modes and simultaneously expanding the scope of substrates, which enriches the toolbox for developing practical P450 biocatalysts for the synthesis of commodity chemicals [
                  - link "154" [ref=e660] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B154-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. However, it is worth noting that there are still some drawbacks that hinder its further industrial use, such as the oxidative damage of P450 caused by the large amounts of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e661]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e662]: "2"
                  - text: ", uncertainty about applying this strategy to other P450s, and the increased cost associated with introducing a large excess of DFSMs. Nevertheless, researchers are actively working on these limitations. Recent efforts by Cong and coworkers have aimed to address these limitations by engineering hydrogen peroxide tunnels in P450 monooxygenases, enabling peroxygenase activity and increasing the H"
                  - subscript [ref=e663]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e664]: "2"
                  - text: "-driven activities of two native NADH-dependent P450 enzymes by >183-fold and >15-fold, respectively ["
                  - link "155" [ref=e665] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B155-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. Moreover, the amount of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e666]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e667]: "2"
                  - text: required for the DFSM-facilitated P450BM3 peroxygenase to obtain the desired product has been reduced by 95–97.5% (with an approximately 95% coupling efficiency) [
                  - link "155" [ref=e668] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B155-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. To further reduce the working concentration of DFSMs, the structure-guided optimization of DFSMs has been conducted, resulting in the identification of some unnatural amino acids with better performances ["
                  - link "16" [ref=e669] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B16-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. This work has greatly enriched the DFSM toolbox for activating the peroxide-shunt pathway of P450s and could provide customized DFSM solutions for specific substrates and reactions."
              - generic [ref=e670]:
                - heading "5. XNAs and nnAAs" [level=2] [ref=e671]: 5. XNAs and nnAAs
                - paragraph [ref=e672]:
                  - text: Besides the cofactors in proteins, various artificial small molecules have been developed for use as building blocks in biomacromolecules. With the development of synthetic biology, it is possible to incorporate non-natural nucleotides (XNAs) and non-natural amino acids (nnAAs) into genes and proteins through genetic manipulation. Due to their higher stability, activity, and excellent anti-degradation ability, some XNAs have been used in quantitative PCR (qPCR) methodology [
                  - link "156" [ref=e673] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B156-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "] and semi-synthetic organism creation. Similarly, more than 200 nnAAs have been incorporated into prokaryotes and eukaryotes ["
                  - link "14" [ref=e674] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B14-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "] and have been used in a variety of explorations, including protein labeling ["
                  - link "157" [ref=e675] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B157-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], biomolecular targeting by reacting with fluorescent probes ["
                  - link "158" [ref=e676] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B158-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "159" [ref=e677] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B159-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "160" [ref=e678] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B160-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], protein interaction analysis ["
                  - link "161" [ref=e679] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B161-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], real-time tracking and in vivo imaging ["
                  - link "162" [ref=e680] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B162-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], biological containment system construction ["
                  - link "163" [ref=e681] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B163-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], the preparation of new biological materials ["
                  - link "164" [ref=e682] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B164-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], lanthipeptides bioengineering ["
                  - link "165" [ref=e683] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B165-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "], and generating new biocatalysts. From this perspective, the incorporation of XNAs and nnAAs expanded the genetic code and central dogma, providing new genetic engineering tools for producing proteins with higher activity, better performance, and wider applications ["
                  - link "166" [ref=e684] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B166-molecules-28-05850"
                  - text: "]. In this section, we will summarize the design of XNAs and the main methods of nnAAs incorporation, focusing on the applications of nnAAs in synthetic biology."
                - generic [ref=e685]:
                  - heading "5.1. XNA Engineering" [level=3] [ref=e686]
                  - paragraph [ref=e687]:
                    - text: Nucleotides consist of bases, five-carbon sugars (ribose or deoxyribose), and phosphate groups, all of which can be modified (
                    - link "Figure 8" [ref=e688] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#molecules-28-05850-f008"
                    - text: ). Base modifications can change the base pairing characteristics and expand the information coding ability of nucleic acids. There are two main strategies for introducing the third base pairs apart from A-T and G-C, called UBPs, into DNA and RNA. The first aims to maintain the Watson–Crick-like hydrogen-bond network between the bases, and the second uses hydrophobic groups to mimic the shape and polarity of the natural bases. The pioneering work of the former came from the Benner laboratory, whose earliest design of isoG-isoC was confirmed to be recognized by DNA and RNA polymerase. However, under the condition of DNA synthesis, iso-C may slowly hydrolyze to U, and the iso-G may exist in the form of tautomerism complementary to U to some extent [
                    - link "167" [ref=e689] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B167-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. To solve these problems, Benner and his team redesigned a new base pair Z:P ["
                    - link "168" [ref=e690] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B168-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "], which could be easily accepted by DNA polymerase, and the retention rate of the non-natural base pair was 97.5% per cycle of PCR amplification ["
                    - link "169" [ref=e691] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B169-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. In contrast to the reassignment of Watson–Crick hydrogen bonds, Kool et al. synthesized the first hydrophobic base pairs F:D, which were shape-like analogs of natural bases for achieving specific pairing through hydrophobicity. They also studied four benzopyrimidine C-nucleosides analogs (xA, xC, xG, xT), and two of them (xA and xC) could be replicated by the polymerase in"
                    - emphasis [ref=e692]: E. coli
                    - text: and produced correct messages [
                    - link "170" [ref=e693] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B170-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. However, it was not clear to what extent the incorporation efficiency was related to the shape complementarity. The idea of hydrophobic base pairing was further developed by Romesberg’s group, who first developed self-complementary pairs of propynyl-isocarbostyril (PICS) to overcome the requirement of shape complementarities ["
                    - link "171" [ref=e694] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B171-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. Subsequently, Romesberg continued to optimize the hydrophobic base pair system, and the resultant 5STIC:NaM pair approached the efficiency of the natural base pair at every step of the replication ["
                    - link "172" [ref=e695] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B172-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. More impressive, Hirao et al. designed a Pa:Ds base pair that could be efficiently amplified by PCR. Based on this, another fluorescent UBP was introduced into a selected site of RNA, and site-specific fluorescent probe tools based on s:Pa and Pa:Ds were established ["
                    - link "173" [ref=e696] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B173-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "], which provided convenience for studying the local structure and intermolecular interaction of RNA. In recent years, researchers have successfully incorporated the hydrophobic base pair TPT3-NaM into"
                    - emphasis [ref=e697]: E. coli
                    - text: . In this semi-synthetic organism, DNA containing these two UBPs was normally transcribed in vivo, resulting in the site-specific incorporation of natural and non-natural amino acids in green fluorescent protein. More importantly, semi-synthetic organisms could grow robustly and maintain the modified genetic material stably [
                    - link "13" [ref=e698] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B13-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]."
                  - figure "Chemical structures of XNAs mentioned in the review. (A) XNA with base modifications; (B) XNA with sugar modifications; (C) XNA with phosphate modifications." [ref=e699]:
                    - heading "Figure 8." [level=4] [ref=e700]
                    - paragraph [ref=e701]:
                      - link "Figure 8 🔍" [ref=e702] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=10421094_molecules-28-05850-g008.jpg
                        - img "Figure 8" [ref=e703]
                        - text: 🔍
                    - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e705] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: figure/molecules-28-05850-f008/
                    - paragraph [ref=e707]:
                      - text: Chemical structures of XNAs mentioned in the review. (
                      - strong [ref=e708]: A
                      - text: ) XNA with base modifications; (
                      - strong [ref=e709]: B
                      - text: ) XNA with sugar modifications; (
                      - strong [ref=e710]: C
                      - text: ) XNA with phosphate modifications.
                  - paragraph [ref=e711]:
                    - text: Besides bases, modifications of sugar rings and phosphate were also reported. The phosphates of threonine nucleic acid (TNA) are attached to oxygen on the 3′ and 2′ sites of furan sugars, and there is no methylene between the sugar ring and the oxygen atom on the phosphate [
                    - link "174" [ref=e712] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B174-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. TNA could be incorporated into DNA templates by Therminator DNA polymerase ["
                    - link "175" [ref=e713] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B175-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. Similarly, locked nucleic acid (LNA) and hexitol nucleic acid (HNA) could also be introduced by different polymerases, such as KOD DNA polymerase, T7 RNA polymerase ["
                    - link "176" [ref=e714] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B176-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "], or Vent (exo-) DNA polymerase ["
                    - link "177" [ref=e715] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B177-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. For phosphates, phosphorothioate was the most common phosphodiester analog; it had mRNA activity and could be recognized by ribosomes ["
                    - link "178" [ref=e716] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B178-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. Boranophosphates and phosphonate were also accepted by polymerases ["
                    - link "179" [ref=e717] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B179-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. The latter contained additional methylene between the 5′ oxygen and phosphorus atoms and was highly resistant to nuclease degradation. It was reported that adenine phosphonate and cytosine phosphonate derivatives were favored by polymerase ["
                    - link "180" [ref=e718] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B180-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. In conclusion, by incorporating XNAs into DNA and RNA, it is possible to improve the expression level and efficiency of the inserted genes due to the stability and resistance to the degradation of XNAs ["
                    - link "181" [ref=e719] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B181-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]."
                - generic [ref=e720]:
                  - heading "5.2. nnAAs Incorporation" [level=3] [ref=e721]
                  - paragraph [ref=e722]:
                    - text: Protein chemical modifications are important tools for elucidating and engineering biological functions, but their applications are limited to 20 natural amino acids. Non-natural amino acids (nnAAs), which can greatly expand protein engineering, are also of great interest. It would be beneficial for the characterization of the protein structure, protein interaction, and protein dynamics by introducing some nnAAs with functional groups. People have developed a variety of methods for incorporating nnAAs in vivo, of which residue-specific incorporation (RSI) and site-specific incorporation (SSI) are the most commonly used. RSI usually uses autotrophic systems for expression hosts to achieve the global substitution of specific natural amino acids, relying on the promiscuity of natural translation mechanisms. This method is simple and convenient and does not require genetic manipulation. However, due to the incorporation of nnAAs at multiple sites, the physicochemical properties of the obtained proteins are often greatly changed [
                    - link "182" [ref=e723] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B182-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. In contrast, SSI causes less disturbance to proteins, but it is extremely challenging to incorporate multiple different nnAAs at the same time, as it requires different genetic modifications."
                  - paragraph [ref=e724]:
                    - text: As shown in
                    - link "Figure 9" [ref=e725] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#molecules-28-05850-f009"
                    - text: ", aminoacyl-tRNA synthetase (aaRS) uses specific anticodons to load amino acids onto transport RNA (tRNA), and then the tRNA charged with amino acids is delivered to the ribosome by elongation factor Tu (EF-Tu) for translation. The translation apparatus is crucial for the incorporation of nnAAs. The selective or specific incorporation of nnAAs into proteins first requires orthogonal aaRS-tRNA pairs. Ideal orthogonal aaRS/tRNA pairs do not cross-react with endogenous amino acids and aaRS/tRNA pairs of host cells but are recognized by host ribosomes ["
                    - link "183" [ref=e726] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B183-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "], such as the orthogonal tyrosyl-tRNA synthetase TyrRS-tRNA"
                    - subscript [ref=e727]: CUA
                    - text: pair from
                    - emphasis [ref=e728]: Methanocaldococcus jannaschii
                    - text: ", the TyrRS-tRNA"
                    - subscript [ref=e729]: CUA
                    - text: and LeuRS-tRNA
                    - subscript [ref=e730]: CUA
                    - text: pairs from
                    - emphasis [ref=e731]: E. coli
                    - text: ", and the pyrrolysyl-tRNA synthetase PylRS-tRNA"
                    - subscript [ref=e732]: CUA
                    - text: pairs from
                    - emphasis [ref=e733]: Methanosarcina barkeri
                    - text: and
                    - emphasis [ref=e734]: Methanosarcina mazei
                    - text: "["
                    - link "184" [ref=e735] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B184-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. During the optimization process of orthogonal aaRS-tRNA pairs, researchers found that the editing domain of aaRS, responsible for hydrolyzing mismatched amino acids, is one of the key targets related to the incorporation efficiency of nnAAs. The T252Y mutation at the active site of the"
                    - emphasis [ref=e736]: E. coli
                    - text: LeuRS enzyme editing domain reduces the editing activity of natural leucine, thus allowing for the introduction of leucine analogs oxonorvaline [
                    - link "185" [ref=e737] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B185-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "] and effectively increasing the incorporation efficiency of nnAAs. Later, Gan et al. ["
                    - link "186" [ref=e738] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B186-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "] simultaneously evolved the anti-codon binding domain of aaRS and the amino acid binding pocket of EF-Tu and adjusted the expression of the evolved translation components in a single vector, further improving the incorporation efficiency of nnAAs. Given the low efficiency of traditional evolutionary techniques and the poor activity and selectivity of the evolved aaRS, Liu et al. ["
                    - link "187" [ref=e739] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B187-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "] evolved a PylRS variant with a 45 times higher catalytic efficiency than that of the wild type and a TyrRS variant with increased selectivity for"
                    - emphasis [ref=e740]: p
                    - text: "-iodo-l-phenylalanine through phage-assisted continuous evolution (PACE)."
                  - figure "Scheme of site-specific incorporation of non-natural amino acids (nnAAs, represented by stars). O-aaRS represents orthogonal aminoacyl tRNA synthetase; nnAA represents non-natural amino acid; O-tRNA represents orthogonal tRNA; EF-Tu represents elongation factor Tu." [ref=e741]:
                    - heading "Figure 9." [level=4] [ref=e742]
                    - paragraph [ref=e743]:
                      - link "Figure 9 🔍" [ref=e744] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=10421094_molecules-28-05850-g009.jpg
                        - img "Figure 9" [ref=e745]
                        - text: 🔍
                    - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e747] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: figure/molecules-28-05850-f009/
                    - paragraph [ref=e749]: Scheme of site-specific incorporation of non-natural amino acids (nnAAs, represented by stars). O-aaRS represents orthogonal aminoacyl tRNA synthetase; nnAA represents non-natural amino acid; O-tRNA represents orthogonal tRNA; EF-Tu represents elongation factor Tu.
                  - paragraph [ref=e750]:
                    - text: In addition to orthogonal aaRS/tRNA pairs and elongation factors, ribosome and release factors (RFs) also significantly affect the incorporation efficiency and specificity of nnAAs.
                    - emphasis [ref=e751]: O
                    - text: "-ribosomes containing O-16S rRNA and anti-Shine-Dalgarno (ASD) sequence mutations (for instance, 5′ GGAGG or 5′ CACAC) were introduced into"
                    - emphasis [ref=e752]: E. coli
                    - text: . The
                    - emphasis [ref=e753]: O
                    - text: "-ribosome could selectively translate orthogonal mRNAs, i.e., O-mRNAs, containing"
                    - emphasis [ref=e754]: O
                    - text: "-ribosome binding sites (such as SD sequence 5′ CCTCC or 5′ GTGTG) but could not translate native mRNA transcripts (SD sequence 5′ GGAGG) ["
                    - link "188" [ref=e755] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B188-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "189" [ref=e756] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B189-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. These modifications were mainly applied to the 16S rRNA of the orthogonal ribosome’s small subunit, while the 23S rRNA of the large subunit was shared by endogenous ribosomes and orthogonal ribosomes. The free exchange of subunits limited the development of orthogonal genetic systems. To further reduce the association with endogenous 16S or 23S subunits, Orelle et al. ["
                    - link "190" [ref=e757] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B190-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "] produced a functional ribosome, Ribo-T, by engineering short rRNA linkers, by which the large and small subunits were covalently tethered into an entity. Because the linking subunits were associated without specificity and mediated translation by association with endogenous subunits, the activity of Ribo-T was still low. Utilizing tether libraries with different sequence lengths and compositions, a new oRibo-T v2/mRNA pair was optimized. Compared with Ribo-T, the growth rate of the new oRibo-T v2 system was increased by 86%, and multiple nnAAs could be incorporated into the synthesized peptides in a site-specific way ["
                    - link "191" [ref=e758] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B191-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. Chin et al. also reported an"
                    - emphasis [ref=e759]: O
                    - text: "-stapled ribosome linked via the optimized RNA staple, which had a similar ability to support cell growth as a natural ribosome ["
                    - link "192" [ref=e760] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B192-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. Nonetheless, the translation system needs to be further optimized to incorporate multiple different nnAAs into the protein simultaneously. With the maturation of genetic code expansion techniques, more blank codons could be used for nnAAs incorporation by reassigning sense or nonsense codons and introducing quadruplet codons and non-natural nucleotides ["
                    - link "193" [ref=e761] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B193-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "], but natural ribosomes recognized tRNAs with quadruplet codons poorly ["
                    - link "194" [ref=e762] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B194-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. Based on the orthogonal ribosome ribo-X ["
                    - link "195" [ref=e763] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B195-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "], Chin and colleagues synthetically evolved ribo-Q1 that was able to take advantage of the quadruplet codons with a similar efficiency and fidelity as a triplet codon. In the presence of Seryl-tRNA synthetase variants/tRNA pairs and MbPylRS-tRNA"
                    - subscript [ref=e764]: CUA
                    - text: ", ribo-Q1 successfully incorporated two different nnAAs into calmodulin GST-CaM-His"
                    - subscript [ref=e765]: "6"
                    - text: "["
                    - link "196" [ref=e766] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B196-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]."
                  - paragraph [ref=e767]:
                    - text: Moreover, the incorporation of nnAAs via the reassignment of nonsense codons (mostly amber codons) was usually limited to peptide chain termination mediated by the release factor RF1. Mukai et al. [
                    - link "197" [ref=e768] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B197-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "] mutated the stop codon (UAG-UAA) of seven essential genes and then successfully deleted RF1; the incorporation efficiency of nnAAs with the amber suppressor tRNA was improved. It is worth mentioning that the addition of RF1-inhibiting antimicrobial peptide apidaecines into the expression medium could also promote the nnAAs entry into"
                    - emphasis [ref=e769]: E. coli
                    - text: BL21 and DH10B without removing the RF1 gene [
                    - link "198" [ref=e770] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B198-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]."
                  - paragraph [ref=e771]:
                    - text: As mentioned above, since each method of incorporating nnAAs (RSI and SSI) has its advantages and disadvantages, it is worth considering a combination of these two approaches—that is, employing orthogonal tRNA/synthetase pairs in autotrophic cells to carry out the site-specific incorporation of nnAAs while making use of endogenous tRNA synthetase to achieve the global incorporation of other nnAAs [
                    - link "199" [ref=e772] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B199-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "200" [ref=e773] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B200-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. Furthermore, cell-free protein synthesis (CFPS) systems are also powerful tools for protein transcription and translation by the use of cell crude extracts or purified OTS components (PURE system) ["
                    - link "201" [ref=e774] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B201-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "202" [ref=e775] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B202-molecules-28-05850"
                    - text: "]. Since there is an open reaction environment lack of living cells, nnAAs can be directly added to the translation reaction without considering the toxicity of orthogonal tRNA/aaRS pairs, which is an important supplement to the above methods. In conclusion, to further improve the incorporation efficiency of nnAAs, the translation mechanism should be considered for coordination optimization as a complex system including codons, tRNAs, aaRSs, EF-Tu, and ribosomes."
                - generic [ref=e776]:
                  - heading "5.3. Applications in Synthetic Biology" [level=3] [ref=e777]
                  - generic [ref=e778]:
                    - heading "5.3.1. Enzymes Engineering" [level=4] [ref=e779]
                    - paragraph [ref=e780]:
                      - text: Improving enzyme activity, stability, and stereoselectivity is a crucial aspect of enzyme engineering. Over the past two decades, numerous nnAAs have been successfully applied to enzyme modification to enhance activity and selectivity (
                      - link "Table 1" [ref=e781] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#molecules-28-05850-t001"
                      - text: ). This has even enabled researchers to discover novel catalytic reactions that are not available in nature [
                      - link "203" [ref=e782] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B203-molecules-28-05850"
                      - text: "]."
                    - generic [ref=e783]:
                      - heading "Enzyme Activity" [level=5] [ref=e784]
                      - paragraph [ref=e785]:
                        - text: ω-transaminase (TAms) is one of the most promising biocatalysts. It has been reported that the incorporation of several nnAAs could enhance the activity of TAm. The Phe88 residue at the active site of TAm was replaced by
                        - emphasis [ref=e786]: p
                        - text: "-benzoylphenylalanine ("
                        - emphasis [ref=e787]: p
                        - text: BzF), which reshaped the size of the active pocket while maintaining hydrophobicity. The resulting TAm variant exhibited significantly improved activity towards 1-phenylpropane-1-amine and benzaldehyde and also broadened the range of substrates [
                        - link "204" [ref=e788] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B204-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]. After replacing tyrosine with 3-fluorotyrosine (mFY) by residue-specific incorporation, the synthesis activity of TAm for ("
                        - emphasis [ref=e789]: S
                        - text: )-1-phenylethylamine was two time higher than that of the wild type in the presence of 20% DMSO (
                        - emphasis [ref=e790]: v/v
                        - text: ) [
                        - link "205" [ref=e791] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B205-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]."
                      - paragraph [ref=e792]:
                        - text: Because
                        - emphasis [ref=e793]: p
                        - text: BzF and other phenylalanine-derived nnAAs are easy to synthesize and have a high incorporation efficiency, they are widely applied in enzyme engineering. The Phe385 of transketolase (TK) played a key role in acceptor substrate binding, and after replacing it with a series of phenylalanine derivatives, the specific activity of the
                        - emphasis [ref=e794]: p
                        - text: "-cyanophenylalanine ("
                        - emphasis [ref=e795]: p
                        - text: CNF) variant to 3-hydroxybenzaldehyde (3-HBA) increased by 43 times [
                        - link "206" [ref=e796] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B206-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]. Another example is"
                        - emphasis [ref=e797]: E. coli
                        - text: nitroreductase (NTR), whose Phe124 was substituted by eight nnAAs such as
                        - emphasis [ref=e798]: p
                        - text: AMF,
                        - emphasis [ref=e799]: p
                        - text: BzF,
                        - emphasis [ref=e800]: p
                        - text: MF,
                        - emphasis [ref=e801]: p
                        - text: tfmF, and
                        - emphasis [ref=e802]: p
                        - text: NF, among which
                        - emphasis [ref=e803]: p
                        - text: NF-NTR had the highest activity against the substrate CB1954 or LH7. It was more than 30 times higher than the natural NTR and more than 2.3 times higher than the optimal natural NTR [
                        - link "207" [ref=e804] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B207-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]. In a reported TEM-1 β-lactamase mutant library with a single nnAA substitution, the"
                        - emphasis [ref=e805]: p
                        - text: "-acrylamido-phenylalanine (AcrF) variant of valine-216 increased its catalytic efficiency by eight times ["
                        - link "208" [ref=e806] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B208-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]. Besides the residues in or near the active site, any changes in the protein structure, even away from the active site, might also have an impact on the catalysis. Substituting four phenylalanine residues that were not near a catalytic site or DNA binding site in restriction endonuclease"
                        - emphasis [ref=e807]: Pvu
                        - text: II with nnAAs, the enzyme activity of
                        - emphasis [ref=e808]: m
                        - text: "-fluorophenylalanine (mFF) variant was twice as high as that of the wild type ["
                        - link "209" [ref=e809] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B209-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]. Additionally, the incorporation of nnAAs was able to be used for the site-specific immobilization of enzymes, which could significantly improve the lifetime and maintain the activity of enzymes during biocatalytic applications. Smith et al. ["
                        - link "210" [ref=e810] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B210-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "] specifically incorporated"
                        - emphasis [ref=e811]: p
                        - text: "-propargyloxy-phenylalanine (pPa) in a specific site of green fluorescent protein (GFP), and the modified GFP was covalent to the superparamagnetic bead via pPa. The immobilized GFP remained active and stable under harsh conditions, including repeated freeze–thaw and incubation at high temperatures in urea."
                      - paragraph [ref=e812]:
                        - text: Along with widely used phenylalanine derivatives, analogs of methionine, cysteine, tyrosine, and histidine have also been shown to enhance enzyme activity. Substituting norleucine (Nle) for methionine in cytochrome P450 peroxidase [
                        - link "211" [ref=e813] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B211-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "] from"
                        - emphasis [ref=e814]: Bacillus megaterium
                        - text: and in lipase [
                        - link "212" [ref=e815] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B212-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "] from"
                        - emphasis [ref=e816]: Thermoanaerobacter thermohydrosulfiricus
                        - text: ", the activity of the variant was increased by 2–10 times. The random incorporation of various sulfhydryl nnAAs into the active sites of"
                        - emphasis [ref=e817]: "N"
                        - text: "-acetylneuraminic acid lyase (NAL) resulted in significant increases in the activity of Phe190Dpc variants regarding the aldol condensation of erythrose and pyruvate ["
                        - link "213" [ref=e818] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B213-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]. Incorporating nnAA l-(7-hydroxycoumarin-4-yl)ethylglycine (Hco) into phosphotriesterase (arPTE) cloned from"
                        - emphasis [ref=e819]: Agrobacterium radiobacter
                        - text: yielded a variant with an 8–11 times higher hydrolysis conversion rate, which was not obtained by natural amino acid optimization [
                        - link "214" [ref=e820] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B214-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]. When"
                        - emphasis [ref=e821]: O
                        - text: "-methyltyrosine ("
                        - emphasis [ref=e822]: OMe
                        - text: Tyr) was integrated into the residues Phe365 and Phe605 of the squalene-hopene cyclase active site, the enzyme activity was improved at low temperatures (<40 °C) [
                        - link "215" [ref=e823] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B215-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]. Tyrosine analogs can also simulate natural post-translational modifications. Lu and colleagues incorporated 2-amino-3-(4-hydroxy-3-(1"
                        - emphasis [ref=e824]: H
                        - text: "-imidazol-1-yl) propanoic acid (ImiTyr) into sperm whale myoglobin, and the Tyr-His crosslinking required for the function of heme-copper oxidase (HCO) was mimicked ["
                        - link "216" [ref=e825] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B216-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]. The HCO functional model Mb_Phe33ImiTyr reduced oxygen three times faster than Mb_Phe33Tyr and released fewer ROS byproducts. In addition, Wang and colleagues introduced synthetic 2-amino-3-(4-hydroxy-3-(methylthio)phenyl)-propanoic acid (3-methylthiotyrosine or MtTyr) ["
                        - link "217" [ref=e826] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B217-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "] to position 33 of myoglobin; the activity of hydroxylamine reductase in the mutant protein was increased."
                      - paragraph [ref=e827]:
                        - text: Excitingly, the incorporation of nnAAs could lead to new protein functions. (2,2′-bipyridin-5-yl) alanine (BpyAla) with a copper ion chelating capacity was incorporated into a noncatalytic catabolite activator protein; in the presence of Cu (II) and 3-mercaptopropionic acid, the recombinant protein acquired the ability to catalyze double-stranded DNA breaking [
                        - link "218" [ref=e828] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B218-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]. Using N"
                        - subscript [ref=e829]: δ
                        - text: "-methyl histidine (NMH) as a non-classical catalytic nucleophile, a hydrolase capable of ester hydrolysis was prepared with the BH32 scaffold protein ["
                        - link "219" [ref=e830] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B219-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]."
                    - generic [ref=e831]:
                      - heading "Enzyme Stability" [level=5] [ref=e832]
                      - paragraph [ref=e833]:
                        - text: Enzyme stability is a crucial factor for determining the practical application of biocatalysis. There are numerous reports on improving stability by the incorporation of nnAAs. Phosphotriesterase (PTE) exhibited a complete loss in structure at 70 °C for 15 min, whereas pFF-PTE incorporated with 4-fluorophenylalanine (pFF) retained its structure by about 30% and showed enhanced refoldability. The melting temperature (Tm) of pFF-PTE was increased by 1.3–2.5 °C [
                        - link "220" [ref=e834] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B220-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]. Thus, fluorination nnAAs provided additional stability and protection against thermal inactivation. Likewise, replacing the tyrosine of ω-aminotransferase with 3-fluorotyrosine (mFY) significantly improved the thermal stability and organic solvent tolerance of the variant as well as the increased catalytic activity ["
                        - link "205" [ref=e835] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B205-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]. The variant retained 36% and 90% of the residual activity at 70 °C and in the presence of 50% ("
                        - emphasis [ref=e836]: v
                        - text: /
                        - emphasis [ref=e837]: v
                        - text: ) dimethyl sulfolone (DMSO), respectively, while the wild-type enzymes showed a corresponding residual activity of only 3.3% and 51%.
                      - paragraph [ref=e838]:
                        - text: Different nnAAs may enhance protein stability in different ways. Thiol-containing nnAAs can form extended disulfide bonds (over ~11 Å) with Cys, breaking the restriction of conventional short disulfide bonds in natural amino acids (the bond length between two β-carbons is approximately 5.5 Å). The incorporation of thiol-containing nnAAs including
                        - emphasis [ref=e839]: O
                        - text: "-(2-mercaptoethyl)-l-tyrosine (SetY),"
                        - emphasis [ref=e840]: O
                        - text: "-(3-mercaptopropyl)-l-tyrosine (SprY), and"
                        - emphasis [ref=e841]: O
                        - text: "-(4-mercaptoethyl)-l-tyrosine (SbuY) allows the melting temperature (Tm) of β-lactamase variants to increase without a loss of catalytic activity ["
                        - link "221" [ref=e842] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B221-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]. In the dimeric"
                        - emphasis [ref=e843]: E. coli
                        - text: homoserine
                        - emphasis [ref=e844]: O
                        - text: "-succinyltransferase ("
                        - emphasis [ref=e845]: metA
                        - text: ), the replacement of Phe21 by
                        - emphasis [ref=e846]: p
                        - text: BzF increased the melting temperature (74 °C) by 21 °C, improving the enzyme stability owing to strengthening the interaction between the monomers [
                        - link "222" [ref=e847] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B222-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]. In another study, 13 kinds of nnAAs were introduced into the lipase (TTL) of"
                        - emphasis [ref=e848]: Thermoanaerobacter thermohydrosulfuricus
                        - text: ", and the results showed that the introduction of nnAAs conferred TTL protective effects against protein denaturant, alkylation, and inhibitory reagents ["
                        - link "223" [ref=e849] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B223-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]. Moreover, the addition of large halogenated nnAAs, i.e., 3-chloro-l-tyrosine and 3-bromo-l-tyrosine, to selected sites of glutathione"
                        - emphasis [ref=e850]: S
                        - text: "-transferase improved thermal stability ["
                        - link "224" [ref=e851] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B224-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "] through the halogen molecules fulfilling the internal space and forming nonstandard stable interactions with neighboring residues."
                    - generic [ref=e852]:
                      - heading "Stereoselectivity and Regioselectivity" [level=5] [ref=e853]
                      - paragraph [ref=e854]:
                        - text: Controlling the stereoselectivity of biocatalysts is a challenging task in protein engineering, and nnAAs may provide a new dimension for achieving this task because some successful studies using nnAAs have been reported. Tryptophan is the largest natural amino acid, and the tryptophan residue at 222 of diketoreductase determines the substrate orientation. The incorporation of nnAAs with side chains larger than the tryptophan, such as
                        - emphasis [ref=e855]: O
                        - text: "-tert-butyl-l-tyrosine (BuOF) and 4-phenyl-l-phenylalanine (BiF) at W222, enhanced enantioselectivity ["
                        - link "225" [ref=e856] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B225-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]. Specifically, wild-type enzymes showed an ("
                        - emphasis [ref=e857]: R
                        - text: )-preference, with an enantiomeric excess (e.e.) of 9.1% for the substrate 2-chloro-1-phenylethanone, while the BuOF variant gave a higher e.e. of 33.7%. Similarly, the incorporation of BpyAla in the selected site of the transcription factor Lactoccocal multidrug resistance regulator (LmrR) could promote enantioselective Friedel–Crafts alkylation [
                        - link "226" [ref=e858] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B226-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "], and the e.e. of its products could reach 83%. Another example was the site-specific incorporation of the tyrosine analogs 3-(2-naphthyl)-alanine (NapA) into an engineered P450 enzyme variant called CYP102A1-139-3 at Ala328, which converted ("
                        - emphasis [ref=e859]: S
                        - text: )-ibuprofen methyl ester into tertiary alcohol with 95% regioselectivity. By comparison, the parent enzyme produced a mixture of benzylic alcohol (62%) and tertiary alcohol (38%). The complete stereoselective oxidation of (+)-nootkatone to 9(
                        - emphasis [ref=e860]: R
                        - text: )-hydroxy-nootkatone was observed when Ala78 was replaced with
                        - emphasis [ref=e861]: p
                        - text: "-acetyl-phenylalanine (pAcF), which was a novel reaction for wild-type enzymes ["
                        - link "227" [ref=e862] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B227-molecules-28-05850"
                        - text: "]."
